--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1 results:**

The champion (score 17264) uses:
- State 0: Repulsion (B=5, rep=3.8064) with a random transition (C=5, p=0.5434) to state 1
- State 1: Spiral (B=6, spd=64) with two transitions: perceive green (C=7, l=2, p=0.3379) back to state 0, and gray floor (C=1, p=0.1255) back to state 0

This makes sense: robots repel each other (spreading out), then spiral to explore, and return to repulsion when they see green walls (wrong strip) or gray floor (middle area). The target is the BLUE-walled strip.

Key insight: Robots should **go to the BLACK floor strip bordered by BLUE walls** and **stay there**. The floor is BLACK in both strips, GRAY in between. The walls are BLUE (target) or GREEN (wrong).

**Strategy:**
- Detect BLUE walls (color 3) → stay/go there
- Avoid GREEN walls (color 2) → move away
- Floor BLACK = in a strip (could be either), GRAY = in the middle

**Plan for 14 machines:**

1. **Survival**: Exact copy of champion
2-6. **Exploitation**: Small edits of champion (tweak probabilities, gains, speeds)
7-14. **Exploration**: Different topologies using blue wall detection, GoToColor behaviors, etc.

For GoToColor toward blue (clr=3) and away from green (clr=2), with floor detection.

Let me design the machines:

**Exploitation variants of champion:**
- Vary rep0 (3.0-5.0), spd1 (50-80), transition probabilities

**Exploration ideas:**
- 3-state: explore → GoToColor(blue) → stop when black floor detected
- Use C=7 with l=3 (blue) to navigate toward target
- GoAwayColor from green (B=9, clr=2)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**

The champion (score 3070) is dramatically better than Round 1's best (17264). It uses:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue, clr=3) → transitions back to itself on BLACK floor (p=0.8) or seeing GREEN (p=0.3)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(green, clr=2) → transitions back to state 0 on GRAY floor (p=0.4) or seeing BLUE (p=0.5)

This is elegant: go toward blue walls → when on black floor (arrived at a strip) stay/reconsider, → if in wrong strip (green visible), run away from green → when back in gray (middle), go toward blue again.

The 2nd place (3080) is nearly identical but adds a Stop state in between. The gap between 2nd (3080) and 3rd (5244) is huge, confirming this pattern is the key.

**Strategy for Round 3:**

1. **Survival**: Exact copy of champion
2. **Exploitation**: Tune probabilities of champion (p values: 0.8→0.7-0.9, 0.3→0.2-0.4, 0.4→0.3-0.5, 0.5→0.4-0.6)
3. **Keep 2nd place** (3-state variant) and tune it
4. **Exploration**: 
   - Add a 3rd transition using C=0 (black floor) to detect being in wrong strip more precisely
   - Try with additional neighbor conditions to coordinate
   - Try variations where state 1 also goes to blue directly

The core logic: GoToBlue (state 0) + GoAwayGreen (state 1) with floor/color-perception transitions between them.

Key parameters to tune:
- In state 0: p0x0 (black floor prob, currently 0.8) - try 0.7, 0.9
- In state 0: p0x1 (see green prob, currently 0.3) - try 0.2, 0.4
- In state 1: p1x0 (gray floor prob, currently 0.4) - try 0.3, 0.5, 0.6
- In state 1: p1x1 (see blue prob, currently 0.5) - try 0.4, 0.6, 0.7

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**

The top 11 machines all score between 3085 and 3356 - a very tight cluster. The champion (3070) from Round 2 is still the best ever. Key observations:

1. All top performers use the same 2-state structure: GoToColor(blue) ↔ GoAwayColor(green)
2. The best new machine (3085) uses p0x0=0.75, p0x1=0.25, p1x0=0.35, p1x1=0.55 - slightly lower than champion
3. Machines that add a 3rd state to stop or add extra transitions perform worse or similarly
4. The machine that goes GoAwayGreen → GoToBlue (reversed starting state, rank 5, score 3205) also works well
5. Adding a 3rd "see blue" transition to state 0 hurts (rank 12, 4253)

**The parameter space seems well-explored for this topology. Let me try:**
- Fine-grained tuning around the best parameters (p0x0 ≈ 0.75-0.80, p0x1 ≈ 0.25-0.30, p1x0 ≈ 0.35-0.45, p1x1 ≈ 0.50-0.60)
- Try very low transition probabilities to make robots "commit" more to each state
- Try different LED colors (shouldn't matter but doesn't hurt)
- Try the same topology but with GoToColor using different speeds or behaviors
- Consider: what if state 1 (GoAwayGreen) transitions to a Stop state on black floor (in target), then only leaves Stop if it sees green?

The big gap between rank 11 (3356) and rank 12 (4253) confirms the 2-state GoToBlue↔GoAwayGreen topology is optimal. Let me focus tight exploitation around the champion parameters while adding a few exploratory variants.

**Champion params**: p0x0=0.80, p0x1=0.30, p1x0=0.40, p1x1=0.50
**Best round 3**: p0x0=0.75, p0x1=0.25, p1x0=0.35, p1x1=0.55

Try grid: p0x0 ∈ {0.72, 0.75, 0.78}, p0x1 ∈ {0.22, 0.25, 0.28}, p1x0 ∈ {0.32, 0.35, 0.38}, p1x1 ∈ {0.50, 0.55, 0.60}

Also try: adding a Stop state when on black and not seeing green (stay in target region).

- p1x0 (gray floor → switch back to GoToBlue): best around 0.34-0.38, champion at 0.37
- p1x1 (see blue → stay in GoAwayGreen): best around 0.53-0.58, champion at 0.53

The differences between scores 3013 and 3256 are small. This may be mostly stochastic noise. I should:
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Fine-tune around champion with very small perturbations (±0.02)
3. Try some slightly different correlation patterns
4. Add a few genuine explorations to escape local optima

Note: the 3-state variant that scored 3093 (same as 5th place) is interesting - it keeps the GoToBlue→Stop→GoAwayGreen structure with similar params. Let me also explore variations where we try:
- Lower p0x1 (less reactive to green while approaching blue)
- Higher p1x1 (more committed to fleeing green)
- Different correlation between the four parameters

Let me also try some genuinely different approaches:
- A 3-state machine with an explicit "stay" state when on black floor without seeing green
- Trying p0x0 much lower (0.5-0.6) to see if robots should be less "hesitant" when on black

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**

The scores are converging tightly (3012-3110 for top 13), with a huge gap to rank 14 (8284). Key observations:

1. Champion: p0x0=0.78, p0x1=0.27, p1x0=0.37, p1x1=0.51
2. 2nd place: p0x0=0.74, p0x1=0.26, p1x0=0.36, p1x1=0.56
3. 3rd place (surprise!): p0x0=0.55, p0x1=0.15, p1x0=0.25, p1x1=0.70 - much lower probabilities throughout, suggesting robots "commit" more to each state
4. The 3-state Stop variant keeps tying with 2-state (rank 9 = same score as rank 8)
5. 3-state machines that route through Stop between GoToBlue and GoAwayGreen score badly (rank 14)

The 3rd place result is interesting: lower p0x0 (0.55 vs 0.78) means robots don't switch away from GoToBlue as often on black floor, and much lower p0x1 (0.15 vs 0.27) means they react less to seeing green while approaching blue. Higher p1x1 (0.70 vs 0.51) means they're more committed to fleeing green when they see blue. This suggests a "commit more" strategy might be worthwhile.

Let me explore:
- Around champion: p0x0∈{0.77,0.78,0.79}, p0x1∈{0.25,0.27,0.29}, p1x0∈{0.35,0.37,0.39}, p1x1∈{0.49,0.51,0.53}
- Around the "low commitment" variant (rank 3): p0x0∈{0.50,0.55,0.60}, p0x1∈{0.10,0.15,0.20}, p1x0∈{0.20,0.25,0.30}, p1x1∈{0.65,0.70,0.75}
- The interpolation between champion and rank 3

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

The champion (3012) still holds. Round 7's best (3013) is essentially tied. The top performers now show:

1. Best: p0x0=0.55, p0x1=0.15, p1x0=0.22, p1x1=0.70
2. Champion: p0x0=0.78, p0x1=0.27, p1x0=0.37, p1x1=0.51
3. p0x0=0.67, p0x1=0.20, p1x0=0.30, p1x1=0.60
4. p0x0=0.58, p0x1=0.16, p1x0=0.26, p1x1=0.66

The scores are extremely tight (3012-3160 across all 13). The parameter landscape appears quite flat. 

**Key observations:**
- The ratio p0x1/p0x0 ≈ 0.27 consistently (champion: 0.27/0.78=0.346, rank1: 0.15/0.55=0.273, rank3: 0.20/0.67=0.299, rank4: 0.16/0.58=0.276)
- The ratio p1x0/p1x1 ≈ 0.31-0.35 consistently (rank1: 0.22/0.70=0.314, champion: 0.37/0.51=0.725 - wait, champion breaks the pattern)
- Actually, for the "low cluster": p1x0 ≈ p1x1/3 to p1x1/3.2

The best result uses p1x0=0.22, p1x1=0.70. Let me try:
- Even lower p1x0 (0.18-0.21) with p1x1=0.70-0.75 in the "low" cluster
- p0x0=0.55-0.57, p0x1=0.14-0.16
- Also try p0x0=0.60-0.65 intermediate range

Since we're in rounds 8-10, I should focus heavily on exploitation of the best regions while including a few diverse explorations.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**

The champion (3012) still holds from Round 5. Round 8's best (3016) uses p0x0=0.56, p0x1=0.15, p1x0=0.22, p1x1=0.70 - very close to champion but in the "low" cluster.

**Key observations:**
- The "low" cluster (p0x0≈0.55-0.56, p0x1≈0.15, p1x0≈0.21-0.22, p1x1≈0.70) consistently scores well
- p0x0=0.56 seems slightly better than 0.55 (rank 1 vs rank 5 with same other params)
- p1x0=0.22 seems better than 0.21 (rank 1 vs rank 2)
- The "high" cluster (0.78, 0.27, 0.37, 0.51) scored last this round (rank 13=3225) - noise!
- The scores across all machines are extremely close (3016-3225), mostly noise

**Round 8 best params**: p0x0=0.56, p0x1=0.15, p1x0=0.22, p1x1=0.70

For Round 9 (penultimate), I should:
1. Keep champion exactly (elitism)
2. Exploit heavily around p0x0=0.56, p0x1=0.15, p1x0=0.22, p1x1=0.70
3. Try small variations: p0x0 ∈ {0.55,0.56,0.57,0.58}, p0x1 ∈ {0.14,0.15,0.16}, p1x0 ∈ {0.20,0.21,0.22,0.23}, p1x1 ∈ {0.68,0.70,0.72}
4. Keep the best "low cluster" variants from recent rounds
5. Add a couple of exploratory machines

The landscape is very flat - differences of ~200 points over score ~3000 are within noise. The champion has survived since Round 5 despite tight competition.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 9 (Final round):**

This is the last round. The champion (3012) still holds from Round 5. Round 9's scores are 3093-3187, all higher than the champion. The landscape is very flat and mostly noise.

**Key parameter observations across all rounds:**
- Best ever: p0x0=0.78, p0x1=0.27, p1x0=0.37, p1x1=0.51
- Round 9 best: p0x0=0.58, p0x1=0.15, p1x0=0.22, p1x1=0.70
- Round 8 best: p0x0=0.56, p0x1=0.15, p1x0=0.22, p1x1=0.70

The "low cluster" (p0x0≈0.55-0.58, p0x1≈0.15, p1x0≈0.22, p1x1≈0.70) consistently performs well but hasn't beaten the champion. The champion's parameters (0.78, 0.27, 0.37, 0.51) come from a different cluster.

**Final round strategy:**
1. Must include exact champion copy
2. Re-try both champion-vicinity and low-cluster variants with some fresh parameter combinations
3. Include some combinations we haven't tried: p0x0=0.59-0.62, p0x1=0.15-0.17, p1x0=0.22-0.24, p1x1=0.68-0.72
4. Try the champion region more carefully: p0x0≈0.76-0.80, p0x1≈0.25-0.28
5. A few genuinely fresh explorations

Given the stochastic nature, re-running similar machines gives different scores each time. The champion score of 3012 was achieved once - let's try to beat it by sampling around both clusters carefully.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
